The first human being named in scripture (Genesis 2:19-20, 5:1-5), and the final name Luke's genealogy of Jesus reaches, run backward past every other ancestor: "the son of Enos, the son of Seth, the son of Adam, the son of God" (3:38). Matthew's competing genealogy stops at Abraham and never reaches this far back; Luke's choice to extend the line to humanity's first common ancestor — and beyond him, explicitly, to God — is read by mainstream scholarship as a deliberate structural claim about the universal scope of Jesus's significance, not a genealogical record extending further than Matthew's simply because Luke had access to more names.

Cited in: Luke 3:38; see Note 45.

[Beat 468]
ANDREW

Apostle, brother of Simon Peter, previously introduced among the called fishermen (Luke 5); named second in Luke's roster of the Twelve (6:14). Beyond his inclusion in all four New Testament apostle lists, there is no independent, extra-biblical corroboration of his individual activity in this period; later Christian tradition assigns him missionary travel and martyrdom, but those accounts surface only centuries later and read as legendary accretion around a bare name.

[Beat 469]
ANNA (PROPHET)

Introduced at Luke 2:36 as "a prophet, the daughter of Phanuel, of the tribe of Asher," who had lived seven years with her husband before his death and, at eighty-four years old, "never left the temple but worshiped there with fasting and prayer night and day" — present when Simeon blesses the infant Jesus, after which she begins speaking about the child to everyone in Jerusalem awaiting redemption (2:36-38). Her tribal designation is a genuinely checkable oddity: Asher was one of the ten northern tribes deported by Assyria in 722 BCE (2 Kings 17:6), after which the historical record loses them as a traceable population; by the first century CE, only Judah, Benjamin, and Levi had any plausible continuous genealogical trail to a specific pre-exilic tribe. Josephus, writing near the same period, still claimed the ten tribes survived intact "beyond the Euphrates" as an uncounted multitude — a claim no outside source has ever verified. Anna's "tribe of Asher" is best read as an inherited family tradition rather than an independently checkable genealogical fact. Raymond Brown's critical commentary groups Anna with Simeon as a literary embodiment of the anawim, the pious poor awaiting Israel's redemption. Appears only in Luke, entirely within this chapter.

Cited in: Luke 2:36-38; see Notes 28, 29, 30; see TRIBE OF ASHER.

[Beat 470]
ANNAS

Former high priest of Jerusalem's Temple, deposed by the Roman prefect Valerius Gratus in 15 CE, nine years into his term — yet Luke still names him alongside the sitting high priest Caiaphas as if both held the office simultaneously (3:2), which by strict Jewish law only one man could. Annas's own family explains the overlap: five of his sons and his son-in-law Caiaphas all held the high priesthood after him in turn, a decades-long family grip on Jerusalem's most powerful religious office that outlasted his own formal removal.

Cited in: Luke 3:2; see Note 39.

[Beat 471]
APOLLONIUS OF TYANA

A first-century CE Greek philosopher and wandering teacher later credited, in a biography written more than a hundred years after his death, with healings, exorcisms, and other wonders broadly comparable to those attributed to Jesus. The long gap between his life and his only detailed written source makes the comparison weaker evidentially than it might first appear [63].

[Beat 472]
ASSARION (COIN)

Small bronze Roman provincial coin, valued at roughly one-sixteenth of a denarius by the first century CE, itself close to a day's unskilled wage. Appears in Luke 12:6 ('five sparrows for two assaria') and its Matthean parallel (10:29, 'two sparrows for one assarion'), quoting different market rates for the same saying [163].

[Beat 473]
ATRAHASIS EPIC

Old Babylonian flood narrative, the older structural source behind the Gilgamesh flood account in Tablet XI; the closer Mesopotamian ancestor to the Noah flood-story genre [223].

[Beat 474]
BARABBAS

Prisoner released in Jesus's place at the crowd's demand (23:18-25); name derives from Aramaic bar-abba, "son of the father" [285].

[Beat 475]
BARTHOLOMEW

One of the Twelve, named in Luke's roster (6:14). Some scholars identify him with 'Nathanael' from John's Gospel, though the identification rests on inference rather than any text stating it directly. No independent, contemporary source corroborates him individually.

[Beat 476]
BARTIMAEUS

Blind beggar healed by Jesus near Jericho per Mark 10:46-52 ("Bartimaeus, son of Timaeus"). Not named in Luke's own account of the parallel healing (18:35-43), where the man is simply "a certain blind man"; the common title "Blind Bartimaeus" applied to Luke 18 is a harmonization with Mark, not Luke's own wording [236].

[Beat 477]
BEELZEBUL / BEELZEBUB

The name applied to "the prince of demons" in the Beelzebul controversy (Luke 11:14-28). The name derives from the Hebrew Bible's "Baal-zebub, the god of Ekron" (2 Kings 1:2-3, 6, 16), consulted by King Ahaziah of Israel after a fall. Rendered "lord of the flies," the name is very likely a deliberate satirical corruption of an authentic divine epithet: Ugaritic texts repeatedly call Baal zbl b'l ars, "Prince, Lord of the Earth" [155]. No archaeological inscription recovered at Ekron itself names this specific deity by either form of the name [156].

[Beat 478]
BETHLEHEM (OF JUDEA)

Named at Luke 2:4 as "the city of David," the small Judean hill-country town roughly eight kilometers south of Jerusalem where Luke has Joseph travel from Nazareth to register for the census, and where Jesus is born. Luke's own reason for naming Bethlehem is explicit within the text: Joseph goes there "because he was of the house and lineage of David" (2:4), tying the birthplace to the prophecy at Micah 5:2 that a ruler for Israel would come from Bethlehem Ephrathah — the same connection Matthew's parallel nativity account makes explicitly when Herod's own scribes cite Micah by name (Matthew 2:5-6). The mainstream critical reading of the two independent Bethlehem claims (Luke's travel narrative and Matthew's assumption that the family already lived there) is that both Gospels independently anchor Jesus's birth to Bethlehem for the same theological reason — fulfilling Micah's prophecy of a Davidic ruler — while every other New Testament reference to Jesus outside these two infancy narratives calls him "Jesus of Nazareth" without qualification or apparent awareness of a Bethlehem birth. Whether the shared Bethlehem claim preserves an actual historical memory or reflects independent theological placement by two authors working from the same prophetic expectation is not resolvable from the evidence available.

Cited in: Luke 2:4, 2:6-7, 2:15; see Notes 16, 18, 22.

[Beat 479]
BETHSAIDA

A fishing town on or near the north shore of the Sea of Galilee, named by Luke as the place Jesus withdrew to before the feeding of the five thousand (9:10). Its exact location is one of the genuinely live disputes in Levantine archaeology: et-Tell, an inland mound about 1.5 kilometers from the current shoreline, was officially declared Bethsaida in 1994 on the strength of excavations led by Rami Arav [127]; a rival excavation at el-Araj, much closer to the water, has produced Roman-period domestic structures and, in 2022, a Byzantine-era Greek inscription invoking the apostle Peter's intercession [130]. Both teams continue to excavate and both continue to argue for their site [128] [129].

This chapter adds: named alongside Chorazin in the same woe-formula (10:13-15), condemned in the same breath as Sodom [140].

[Beat 480]
BOOK OF TOBIT

A Jewish deuterocanonical/apocryphal narrative text, generally dated to the 2nd century BCE, whose ethical instruction 'Do not do to anyone what you yourself would hate' (4:15) is the earliest clearly datable written parallel to both Hillel's later-attributed saying and Luke's Golden Rule [91].

[Beat 481]
CAESAR AUGUSTUS

Named at Luke 2:1 as the emperor whose decree "that all the world should be registered" sets the nativity's chronology in motion — Rome's first emperor, born Gaius Octavius, who ruled from 27 BCE until his death in 14 CE after defeating Mark Antony and Cleopatra and ending a century of civil war. "All the world" translates the Greek oikoumene, a term Roman writers used for the empire itself rather than the literal globe; Augustus is independently and extensively documented through his own surviving public writings (the Res Gestae Divi Augusti, his autobiographical account of his reign, inscribed on temple walls across the empire and still readable today) and through Roman historians including Suetonius and Tacitus. What is not independently attested anywhere outside Luke is the specific decree Luke describes: no surviving Roman source records a single, empire-wide registration ordered by Augustus in one year. Augustus is genuinely associated with expanding provincial census-taking as an administrative practice during his reign, but the actual practice was local and staggered rather than simultaneous — Egypt's own surviving census cycle, for example, recurred roughly every fourteen years, administered province by province, not empire-wide in a single stroke.

Cited in: Luke 2:1; see Notes 16, 18, 19, 22.

[Beat 482]
CAESAREA PHILIPPI

A Roman city near the springs that feed the Jordan River, in the tetrarchy of Philip the Tetrarch, named by Matthew and Mark as the setting for Peter's confession. Luke's parallel account (9:18-20) does not name this or any location [133].

[Beat 483]
CAIAPHAS

The sitting high priest during John's and Jesus's entire adult ministry (18-36 CE), named alongside his father-in-law and predecessor Annas in Luke's opening synchronism (3:2). Appointed by the Roman prefect Valerius Gratus, Caiaphas held the office longer than almost any other first-century high priest, a tenure spanning nearly the whole period covered by this Gospel.

Cited in: Luke 3:2; see Note 39.

[Beat 484]
CAPERNAUM

A fishing village on the northwest shore of the Sea of Galilee that becomes Jesus's operational base for much of his Galilean ministry, first entered in this chapter (4:31). Archaeology at the site is unusually rich but also unusually contested: the visible white-limestone synagogue building dates to the fourth or fifth century CE, and whether basalt remains beneath it preserve an actual first-century synagogue is a live, unresolved dispute among the site's own excavators [58]. A separate excavated structure nearby, beneath a fifth-century octagonal church, is a strong (though not certain) candidate for the historical `House of Peter'' referenced in this chapter's healing of Simon's mother-in-law [59].

This chapter adds: excavation revealed basalt-block domestic architecture consistent with the roof-access detail in 5:19 [73], and a customs house consistent with the town's position on an internal Herodian tetrarchy border, astride a trade route toward Damascus [77].

This chapter adds: a black basalt synagogue foundation, dated by associated first-century pottery, is a plausible physical trace of the synagogue this chapter's centurion is said to have built (7:5) [96].

This chapter adds: named in the same woe-triad as Chorazin and Bethsaida (10:15), a useful comparative-footprint point once Capernaum's own better-attested first-century archaeology is considered.

This chapter adds the domestic-architecture claim (single-room, shared-courtyard housing, whole family sleeping together) underlying the friend-at-midnight parable [154].

[Beat 485]
CHORAZIN

A Galilean village roughly two and a half miles north of the Sea of Galilee, named by Jesus alongside Bethsaida and Capernaum in a formula of judgment against towns that saw his works and did not repent (10:13). Excavated across the twentieth century, the site preserves a basalt-built village of about twenty-five acres centered on a monumental synagogue — but that synagogue dates to the late third or early fourth century CE, and excavators have not confirmed an undisputed first-century occupation layer beneath it [139].

[Beat 486]
CHUZA

Named at Luke 8:3 as epitropos, "steward" or "manager," in the court of Herod Antipas, and as the husband of Joanna. No extra-biblical record of a specific individual named Chuza has been identified; the title itself is independently attested as a genuine Greco-Roman household office [108].

[Beat 487]
CIRCUMVALLATION WALL (SIEGE OF JERUSALEM, 70 CE)

A 39-stadia siege wall studded with thirteen forts, built by Titus's Roman forces around Jerusalem in three days per Josephus's eyewitness account, sealing the city to starve its defenders [263]. The closest independent, dated military correlate to Luke 21:20's "compassed with armies" language.

[Beat 488]
CLAUDIUS FAMINE

A famine affecting Judea and other parts of the Roman world during the reign of Emperor Claudius (c. 45-47 CE), independently attested by Suetonius, Tacitus, and Josephus; relieved in Jerusalem by Queen Helena of Adiabene's grain shipments [261] [262]. Widely identified as the background event behind Agabus's prophecy in Acts 11:28.

[Beat 489]
CLEOPAS

One of the two disciples encountered by the risen Jesus on the road to Emmaus (24:18); the only one of the pair Luke names, and otherwise unattested outside this pericope.

[Beat 490]
CODEX BEZAE

Fifth-century Greek-Latin diglot manuscript, the primary witness to the "Western" New Testament text-type; key to the "Western non-interpolations" debate over several shorter readings in Luke 24 [301].

[Beat 491]
CODEX BEZAE (WESTERN TEXT)

A fifth-century bilingual (Greek-Latin) New Testament manuscript, the principal witness to the "Western" textual tradition, notable for a cluster of shorter or reordered readings, including at Luke 22:17-20 [269].

[Beat 492]
CODEX SINAITICUS

Fourth-century manuscript, one of the two oldest surviving complete Greek New Testaments; its original hand omits "and was carried up into heaven" at Luke 24:51, a reading later added by a corrector [301].

[Beat 493]
CONFUCIUS

Chinese philosopher (traditionally 6th-5th century BCE), whose Analects articulate an independent negative-reciprocity ethic paralleling Luke's Golden Rule with no plausible line of direct transmission between the two traditions [92].

[Beat 494]
CRITERION OF EMBARRASSMENT

Historical-Jesus research tool, formalized by John P. Meier, holding that details awkward or counterproductive for early Christian storytellers to invent are more likely to reflect authentic memory [296].

[Beat 495]
CUMANUS

Roman procurator of Judea, circa 48-52 CE, named by Josephus as the official who allegedly took bribes to avoid punishing Samaritans after they killed Galilean pilgrims, an inaction that triggered further Galilean-Samaritan violence [136].

[Beat 496]
CYRENE

North African Greek city (modern Libya) with an attested Jewish community, one of four recognized population classes per Strabo [286].

[Beat 497]
DAVID

Invoked twice in Luke 1 as the ancestral throne Jesus is prophesied to inherit: Joseph is identified as being "of the house of David" (1:27), and Gabriel tells Mary that her son will receive "the throne of his ancestor David" and "reign over the house of Jacob forever" (1:32-33) — language drawing directly on the dynastic promise made to David in 2 Samuel 7:12-16, that his line would hold the throne without end. Zechariah's own hymn, the Benedictus, later in the same chapter invokes David again, praising God for having "raised up a horn of salvation for us in the house of his servant David" (1:69). Unlike Matthew, which opens with a full generation-by-generation genealogy tracing this line (Matthew 1:1-17), Luke's chapter 1 asserts the Davidic connection through direct angelic speech rather than a documented lineage; Luke supplies his own genealogy later, at 3:23-38, tracing a different line of descent than Matthew's between David and Joseph, a divergence this project's genealogies-of-jesus grounding document treats at length. The 1993 discovery of the Tel Dan Stele, an Aramaic inscription referencing a "House of David" from roughly a century after David's traditional reign, remains the earliest and most substantial evidence outside the biblical text itself that a dynasty using David's name was a real, historically known royal house in the region — independent of, and considerably narrower than, any of the specific claims either Gospel makes about a descendant of that house centuries later.

Cited in: Luke 1:27, 1:32-33, 1:69.

[Beat 498]
DECAPOLIS

A loose league of roughly ten Hellenized, largely Gentile cities on the eastern and southeastern fringe of Galilee, including Gerasa and Gadara [119]. Its Gentile-majority character explains the commercial pig-herding in the Gerasene demoniac episode.

[Beat 499]
DENARIUS (TRIBUTE PENNY)

Roman silver coin bearing Tiberius's portrait and a divine-filiation inscription (TI CAESAR DIVI AVG F AVGVSTVS), at the center of the "render unto Caesar" episode (20:20-26) [254].

[Beat 500]
DROPSY (HYDROPS)

Ancient Greek medical term (hydropikos/hydrops, from hydor, "water") for generalized bodily swelling from fluid retention, discussed as a recognized diagnostic category in the Hippocratic Corpus [187]. A real ancient clinical label that survived in English medical usage into the nineteenth century before being superseded by modern understanding of edema's specific causes.

[Beat 501]
EKRON

One of the five principal cities of the Philistine pentapolis, identified with the site of Tel Miqne. Named as the home of the god "Baal-zebub" consulted by King Ahaziah (2 Kings 1:2). Excavations uncovered a monumental Iron Age II temple complex and, in 1996, a royal dedicatory inscription naming five generations of Ekron rulers and dedicating the temple to the goddess "Ptgyh" — independently confirming Ekron's reality and prominence, though the inscription postdates the 2 Kings narrative by roughly two centuries and does not itself name Baal-zebub [156].

[Beat 502]
ELEAZAR (EXORCIST)

A Jewish exorcist described by the first-century historian Flavius Josephus as an eyewitnessed contemporary, who performed an exorcism using a ring, a root, and invocations of Solomon's name in the presence of the Roman commander (later emperor) Vespasian and his officers. Cited in this chapter as independent, non-Christian evidence that exorcism was a recognized, practiced social role in first-century Judea [61].

This chapter's "strange exorcist" episode (Luke 9:49-50) reinforces the point that unaffiliated Jewish exorcists invoking a powerful name to command spirits were a recognized type in the period, not a uniquely Christian phenomenon.

[Beat 503]
ELIJAH

Prophet of the northern kingdom of Israel who, uniquely among biblical figures, is described as taken up bodily in a whirlwind rather than dying (2 Kings 2:11), feeding a live expectation in Second Temple Judaism of his eschatological return. Appears with Moses at the Transfiguration (9:30).

[Beat 504]
ELIZABETH

Introduced at Luke 1:5-7 as Zechariah's wife, herself "descended from the priestly line of Aaron," described as righteous before God but "barren" and "getting on in years" — the specific pairing of piety and childlessness that marks her, for a reader familiar with the Hebrew Bible, as standing in the recognized lineage of biblical barren-wife narratives: Sarah, Rebekah, Rachel, Manoah's wife, and Hannah, each granted a son central to Israel's story only after years of childlessness and a divine announcement [8]. Gabriel's news that she will bear a son in her old age (1:13-17) is fulfilled at 1:24-25, and at 1:39-45 her still-unborn child "leaps" in recognition when the pregnant Mary visits her, prompting Elizabeth's own prophetic greeting before Mary says anything about her condition. She insists on the name "John" over her relatives' objection that no one in the family carries that name (1:60-61), a small scene that only functions as narrated surprise because naming a son after his father or another honored relative was the default first-century convention it departs from [13]. Called Mary's "relative" (syngenis, 1:36) — a term of kinship broad enough that no fixed genealogical relationship (cousin, more distant relation) can be established from the Greek alone. Appears only in Luke, entirely within chapter 1.

Cited in: Luke 1:5-25, 1:36-45, 1:57-63; see Notes 8, 13.

[Beat 505]
EMMAUS

Village named as the destination of the road encounter in Luke 24:13-35, "about sixty stadia" from Jerusalem in most manuscripts. Its precise identity is disputed among three candidate sites, a debate rooted in a genuine manuscript disagreement over the stated distance itself [297].

[Beat 506]
EPICTETUS

Stoic-school philosopher (c. 50-135 CE) whose recorded Discourses identify the staff, wallet, and cloak as the defining, recognizable equipment of a Cynic philosopher of the period [125] — the same items Jesus's mission instructions in Luke 9:3 specifically forbid the apostles to carry.

[Beat 507]
FLAVIUS JOSEPHUS

The first-century Jewish historian (c. 37-100 CE) whose two major works, The Jewish War and Jewish Antiquities, supply nearly all of the independent documentary evidence this chapter checks Luke's nativity chronology against. Born Yosef ben Matityahu into a priestly family in Jerusalem, Josephus served as a Jewish commander during the First Jewish-Roman War before surrendering to Rome, later gaining Roman citizenship and imperial patronage under the Flavian emperors (hence "Flavius"), under whose sponsorship he wrote his histories in Rome. His account of Herod the Great's death, dated via a lunar eclipse to c. 4 BCE, and his account of Quirinius's Judean census, dated to 6 CE, are the two fixed points that create the roughly decade-long gap between Matthew's and Luke's own nativity chronologies. Josephus wrote decades after the events of Luke 2, with his own political and apologetic interests (defending Judaism to a Roman readership, and himself, to history) — a fact worth naming rather than treating him as a neutral source, though his outside corroboration remains the single most substantial body of independent evidence available for the entire period covered by this project.

Cited in: Notes 16, 17, 29.

This chapter adds: his Jewish War (4.160-161) is the earliest source to use 'Zealots' as an organized faction's proper name, and he doesn't apply the term until his narrative reaches 66-68 CE, a generation-plus after Luke 6's setting [87].

This chapter cites a specific, precisely-located passage (Antiquities of the Jews 18.5.2, section 116-119) and its content regarding John the Baptist's imprisonment motive, distinct from any prior Josephus citation in this book.

This chapter adds: Antiquities 20.9.1 records the 62 CE stoning of "James, the brother of Jesus who was called Christ," independent non-Gospel attestation of a named sibling of Jesus [111].

This chapter adds two citations: his account of personally fortifying Mount Tabor ahead of the First Jewish Revolt (Jewish War 4.54-61) [134] and his account of the Samaritan-Galilean pilgrim conflict under the procurator Cumanus (Antiquities 20.118-136) [136].

This chapter adds two citations: Antiquities 20.118 (the Ginea attack on Galilean pilgrims, documenting Samaritan-Jewish hostility) [144] and Jewish War 4.8 (the Jordan Valley/Jericho terrain description and distance figures) [141].

This chapter cites his testimony on the Pharisee/Sadducee resurrection debate (Antiquities 18.14, 18.16; Jewish War 2.163-165) [189].

This chapter adds citations: Antiquities 20.9.5 and Jewish War 2.14 on procurators Albinus and Florus [227]; Antiquities 15.4.2 and Jewish War 4.459-475 on the Jericho balsam economy [238].

This chapter adds: Antiquities of the Jews 4.8.15 (=4.219), his explicit statement on the inadmissibility of women's legal testimony, cited as the independent-record backdrop to the empty-tomb "argument from embarrassment" (24:1-12) [293].

[Beat 508]
GABRIEL

The angel who appears twice in Luke 1: first to Zechariah in the Temple sanctuary, announcing John's birth (1:11-20), and again six months later to Mary in Nazareth, announcing Jesus's birth (1:26-38) — the two annunciations built as deliberate companion scenes, with Zechariah's doubt answered by silence and Mary's question ("How can this be?", 1:34) answered instead with an explanation. Gabriel names himself directly at 1:19 — "I am Gabriel, who stands in the presence of God" — the only time in Luke's Gospel an angelic messenger identifies himself by name to a human character. Gabriel appears elsewhere in scripture only in the Hebrew Bible's book of Daniel, where he interprets Daniel's visions and delivers a prophecy of a fixed span of "weeks" (years) before a coming deliverance (Daniel 8:15-27, 9:20-27) — the only angel named in either the Hebrew Bible or the New Testament aside from Michael, and the direct link between Luke's infancy narrative and apocalyptic literature's own visionary-messenger convention. Nothing in the independent historical or archaeological record can speak to angelic beings one way or another; this entry describes the text's own internal presentation, not an external evidentiary claim.

Cited in: Luke 1:11-20, 1:26-38.

[Beat 509]
GALILEE

Identified at Luke 1:26 as the region containing Nazareth, the town Gabriel visits Mary in — the northern portion of Roman-era Palestine, governed in this period, after Herod the Great's death, as the separate tetrarchy of his son Herod Antipas (4 BCE-39 CE), administratively distinct from Judea to the south for the entire span both Gospels' narratives cover. Luke's chapter 1 keeps its two settings carefully separated by this same regional line: Zechariah and Elizabeth live in "the hill country of Judea" (1:39), while Mary and Joseph are in Galilee, and Mary's journey to visit Elizabeth (1:39-40) crosses between the two regions — a real geographic and administrative boundary, not an incidental narrative detail. Galilee in this period contained a genuine mix of small agricultural villages like Nazareth alongside larger, more Hellenized urban centers under active development, above all Sepphoris, a contrast this project's discussion of Nazareth's own archaeology treats directly [11].

Cited in: Luke 1:26; see Note 11.

This chapter adds: archaeologically confirmed first-century synagogues at Gamla and Magdala independently corroborate synagogue assembly as a real, contemporary Galilean/Golan institution rather than a later literary invention [85].

This chapter adds: documented site of Roman-period absentee-landlord estate agriculture (Jezreel Valley), the economic backdrop assumed by the wicked-tenants parable [253].

[Beat 510]
GALLICINIUM

Latin name for the third of the four Roman night watches (roughly midnight-3 a.m.), literally "cockcrow"; used conventionally for that time span independent of an actual rooster [279].

[Beat 511]
GAMLA

A Jewish town on a steep ridge in the Golan Heights, site of one of the earliest archaeologically confirmed purpose-built synagogues (1st century BCE-1st century CE), destroyed by Rome in 67 CE during the Jewish Revolt [85].

[Beat 512]
GERASA

A major Hellenized city of the Decapolis, modern Jerash in Jordan, located roughly 55 kilometers southeast of the Sea of Galilee [116]. Appears in the best manuscripts as the site of the demoniac's healing (8:26), a reading most textual critics regard as a scribal substitution for the smaller, correctly-located Gergesa [115].

[Beat 513]
GERGESA (KURSI)

A small site on the Sea of Galilee's eastern shore, identified since the Byzantine period as the likely actual location of the Gerasene demoniac story, on the strength of being the only stretch of that shoreline with a steep bank matching the narrative [117].

[Beat 514]
GET

The Jewish bill of divorce, rooted in Deuteronomy 24:1's "write, hand, send away" formula; physical examples survive from the fifth century BCE (Elephantine) through the first and second centuries CE (Wadi Murabba'at, the Babatha archive) [212] [210].

[Beat 515]
GRIFFON VULTURE (NESHER)

Gyps fulvus, the obligate-carrion-feeding raptor most likely meant by the "vultures" of Luke 17:37; distinct in feeding ecology from the eagle later translators sometimes substituted [224].

[Beat 516]
HANINA BEN DOSA

A first-century CE Galilean Jewish holy man remembered in rabbinic sources as a healer and `man of deeds,'' roughly a contemporary of Jesus; the Mishnah states that the class of wonder-workers he represented lost their powers around the time of the Second Temple's destruction [64].

[Beat 517]
HELVIDIUS

A fourth-century Christian writer whose own treatise survives only through Jerome's rebuttal. Helvidius argued for the plain-sense reading of Jesus's "brothers" as Mary's own subsequent children by Joseph [113].

[Beat 518]
HEMATOHIDROSIS

A rare, medically documented condition in which blood is expressed through intact sweat glands under extreme stress; relevant to but not a confirmed explanation of Luke 22:44's simile "like drops of blood" [275].

[Beat 519]
HEROD ANTIPAS

The "Herod tetrarch of Galilee" of Luke 3:1 — not Herod the Great, but one of his sons, who inherited only a quarter of his father's kingdom (Galilee and Perea) in the 4 BCE division and never held the title of king. Antipas is the ruler behind John the Baptist's arrest, narrated later in this same chapter (3:19-20): Luke names Herodias, Antipas's brother's wife, as the proximate cause, matching Matthew and Mark, while Josephus's own independent account gives a more political motive — fear of John's popular following tipping into rebellion. Antipas's own reign ended in 39 CE, when his nephew Herod Agrippa I had him denounced to the emperor Caligula as disloyal; he died in exile.

Cited in: Luke 3:1, 3:19-20; see Note 41.

This chapter adds: made Sepphoris his first Galilean capital and drove a major Roman-style construction boom across roughly Jesus's boyhood and early adulthood [93].

This chapter adds: maintained his own independent, non-Roman-legionary military forces organized along Roman lines, meaning 'centurion' references to his territory (Luke 7:1-10) likely denote an officer in his own service, not a literal Roman soldier [94] [95].

This chapter adds: maintained a formal household administration headed by an epitropos (Chuza), evidencing a functioning court bureaucracy [108].

This chapter adds the "that fox" characterization (13:31-32), with the Bivin argument that the idiom connotes insignificance rather than cunning [185].

This chapter adds: the Luke-exclusive trial scene (23:6-12), with no parallel in Mark, Matthew, or John, and the Pilate-Herod reconciliation note (23:12) cross-referenced to the Galileans-killed-by-Pilate incident covered in Chapter 13 [282].

[Beat 520]
HEROD ARCHELAUS

Herod the Great's son and heir to Judea; traveled to Rome to have his kingship confirmed by Augustus over Jewish opposition; a historical parallel proposed for the harsh nobleman of the parable of the ten minas [243] [244].

[Beat 521]
HEROD THE GREAT

Named only in passing at Luke 1:5 — "In the days of King Herod of Judea" — as the chronological marker establishing when Zechariah's priestly service and Gabriel's first annunciation take place; he plays no further role anywhere in Luke's chapter 1 and is a different figure from his son Herod Antipas, the tetrarch of Galilee who appears later in Luke's narrative (3:1, 3:19-20) and whom this Gospel's own Passion account eventually brings into direct contact with Jesus (23:6-12). Herod the Great's reign as Rome's client king over Judea is independently dated, chiefly from Josephus's detailed political narrative, to 37-4 BCE — a client kingship, not full sovereignty, confirmed by coinage, building projects (including the Jerusalem Temple's own massive Herodian expansion, the same Temple Zechariah is serving in), and Roman administrative sources external to scripture. Luke's placement of these events "in the days of King Herod" situates the annunciations to Zechariah and Mary within the final years of that reign, consistent with, though not independently datable beyond, the same general period Matthew's own nativity chronology assumes.

Cited in: Luke 1:5.

[Beat 522]
HERODIAS

Wife of Herod Antipas (previously married to his brother, per the Gospels' account) and, in the Gospels' telling, the reason John the Baptist publicly condemned Antipas and was imprisoned. This chapter's discussion of 7:18-35 notes the genuine divergence between the Gospels' personal/moral rationale for John's imprisonment and Josephus's political rationale [100].

[Beat 523]
HILLEL THE ELDER

A leading Pharisaic sage active in the generation immediately preceding Jesus, remembered in later rabbinic tradition for summarizing the Torah in negative-reciprocity terms strikingly close to Luke's Golden Rule (6:31), though the anecdote survives only in the Babylonian Talmud, redacted centuries after his lifetime [91].

This chapter adds: Mishnah Gittin 9:10 records Beit Hillel's position that a husband may divorce his wife for any deficiency at all, "even if she spoiled his food" — the lenient pole of the divorce debate Luke 16:18 arguably addresses [208] [209].

[Beat 524]
HONI THE CIRCLE-DRAWER

A first-century BCE Jewish charismatic holy man remembered in rabbinic tradition chiefly as a successful rainmaker. Cited by historian Geza Vermes as part of a recognized regional category of Galilean-adjacent Jewish wonder-workers that Jesus's reported healing and exorcism activity in this chapter resembles [64].

[Beat 525]
INFANCY GOSPEL OF THOMAS

A noncanonical Greek text, dated by scholarly consensus to the second century CE, that narrates a series of childhood miracles attributed to Jesus between roughly ages five and twelve — including molding clay sparrows into living birds and cursing a boy who disturbed his rainwater pools, causing the boy to wither and die. Distinct from the canonical apostle Thomas and from the separate, unrelated Gospel of Thomas sayings collection. It survives in Greek along with Syriac, Arabic, Ethiopic, Slavonic, and other translations, evidence it was widely copied across the ancient Mediterranean and Near East — yet the mainstream church never received it into the New Testament canon, and no reputable historian treats its episodes as attesting real events. It exists as a useful, real contrast case for this chapter's own closing pericope: the canonical Gospels are entirely silent about Jesus's life between infancy and the start of his adult ministry, with the single exception of Luke's own boy-at-the-Temple scene (2:41-52) — while the Infancy Gospel of Thomas does claim detailed knowledge of exactly that silent period, and was rejected anyway, by the same believing communities that could have canonized it had they judged it credible. The gap between a claim being attested somewhere and a claim being accepted as scripture or history could hardly be drawn more plainly.

Cited in: (referenced against Luke 2:39-52); see Note 33.

[Beat 526]
ISAIAH (PROPHET)

The eighth-century BCE Judean prophet whose scroll Jesus is handed in the Nazareth synagogue and reads from (4:17-19), quoting a composite of Isaiah 61:1-2 with an inserted clause from Isaiah 58:6 and an omitted clause from 61:2 itself [51] [52].

This chapter's Rich Fool beat cites Isaiah 22:13 ('let us eat and drink, for tomorrow we die') as an Old Testament comparative parallel to the "eat, drink, be merry" carpe diem commonplace [169].

This chapter adds: Isaiah 5:1-7 (Song of the Vineyard) is the direct literary source echoed almost verbatim by the Parable of the Wicked Tenants' opening (20:9) [251].

[Beat 527]
ISOCRATES

Athenian rhetorician and educator (436-338 BCE), whose advice to King Nicocles articulates a near-identical negative-reciprocity formula to Confucius's and to Luke's Golden Rule, independently of both [92].

[Beat 528]
JAIRUS

Named at Luke 8:41 as an archisynagogos, "leader of the synagogue," whose twelve-year-old daughter Jesus raises. The title is independently and epigraphically attested by the pre-70 CE Theodotos inscription from Jerusalem [124].

[Beat 529]
JAMES (BROTHER OF JESUS / JAMES THE JUST)

Distinct from the apostles James son of Zebedee and James son of Alphaeus already covered in this book's glossary, this James is identified in Luke 8:19-21 (implicitly, among the unnamed "brothers") and independently in Paul's letters and Josephus as Jesus's biological brother. Paul calls him "the Lord's brother" (Galatians 1:19) [112]; Josephus records his stoning by order of the high priest Ananus in 62 CE [111].

[Beat 530]
JAMES (SON OF ALPHAEUS)

One of the Twelve, named in Luke's roster (6:15), distinguished from James son of Zebedee by patronymic. Sometimes conflated in later tradition with 'James the Just,' leader of the Jerusalem church mentioned by Josephus and Paul, though this identification is disputed and not made by Luke's text itself.

[Beat 531]
JAMES (SON OF ZEBEDEE)

One of the two fishing partners of Simon named in this chapter (5:10), called alongside his brother John at the same lakeside episode. Distinguished in later Christian tradition from James the brother of Jesus and from James — son of Alphaeus. The sons-of-Zebedee fishing operation Luke describes fits the documented pattern of family-based Galilean fishing partnerships that pooled boats, nets, and labor [68].

This chapter adds: named in Luke's roster of the Twelve (6:14) as part of the inner circle of three (with Peter and John); his later death by execution under Herod Agrippa I is independently datable via Acts 12:2.

New characterization: at 9:54, he and his brother John ask Jesus for authority to call down fire on the Samaritan village that refused them, echoing 2 Kings 1:10 — evidence of the "Sons of Thunder" temperament.

[Beat 532]
JEBEL QURUNTUL (MOUNT OF TEMPTATION)

A mountain above Jericho in the West Bank, identified since at least the fourth century CE by Christian pilgrimage tradition as the site of Jesus's wilderness temptation (4:1-13), with a monastery established there in the sixth century. The identification is not made by the biblical text itself, which names no location for the temptation scene [49].

[Beat 533]
JERICHO

An ancient city in the Jordan Valley, at roughly 258 meters below sea level among the lowest permanently inhabited elevations on Earth, and the destination of the traveler in the Good Samaritan parable (10:30). Jerusalem sits roughly a thousand meters higher over a road of some seventeen to eighteen miles [141].

This chapter adds two new checkable claims: Herod the Great's excavated winter palace complex [237] and the city's documented balsam/date agricultural economy [238], offered as economic backdrop for 18:35-43.

This chapter adds the Zacchaeus/architelones episode and the sycamore-fig botanical detail tied to this location [239] [241].

[Beat 534]
JEROME

Fourth-century Christian scholar and translator of the Latin Vulgate, author of Adversus Helvidium (ca. 383 CE), a treatise defending Mary's perpetual virginity by arguing the Gospels' "brothers" of Jesus were cousins or broader kin [113].

[Beat 535]
JETHRO

Midianite priest and father-in-law of Moses, whose counsel in Exodus 18:21 to organize Israel into units of tens, fifties, hundreds, and thousands is the likely administrative background for Jesus's instruction to seat the crowd "in groups of about fifty" (9:14) [132].

[Beat 536]
JEZREEL VALLEY

Fertile Galilean plain documented as a site of large Roman-period absentee-owned agricultural estates [253].

[Beat 537]
JOANNA (WIFE OF CHUZA)

Named at Luke 8:3 as one of the women who traveled with and financially supported Jesus's ministry. Identified by her marriage to Chuza, Herod Antipas's epitropos [108].

This chapter adds: named in Luke's empty-tomb witness list (24:10) but absent from the parallel lists in Mark and Matthew — a specific, checkable synoptic-divergence data point [295].

[Beat 538]
JOHN (SON OF ZEBEDEE)

James's brother and fishing partner, called together with him in this chapter (5:10). Not to be confused with John the Baptist. Later Christian tradition identifies him with the author of a Gospel and Johannine epistles bearing his name, an identification this chapter's material does not itself address.

This chapter adds: named in Luke's roster of the Twelve (6:14) as part of the same inner three; his later association with the Fourth Gospel and Johannine letters is a separate and much-debated authorship question.

New characterization: at 9:54, he and his brother James ask Jesus for authority to call down fire on the Samaritan village that refused them, echoing 2 Kings 1:10 — evidence of the "Sons of Thunder" temperament.

[Beat 539]
JOHN THE BAPTIST

Luke 1 narrates John's birth, not his adult ministry: Gabriel announces his coming to Zechariah in the Temple (1:11-20), describing him in advance as one who will be "great in the sight of the Lord," abstain from wine, be "filled with the Holy Spirit" from birth, and "turn many of the people of Israel to the Lord their God" in "the spirit and power of Elijah" (1:15-17) — language that deliberately recalls the Hebrew Bible's own prophecy of Elijah's return (Malachi 4:5-6) before John has said or done anything on the page. He is born to Zechariah and Elizabeth in their old age, named "John" over the family's objection that no relative bears that name (1:57-63), and the chapter closes noting only that "the child grew and became strong in spirit, and he was in the wilderness until the day he appeared publicly to Israel" (1:80) — a single summarizing verse that skips his entire childhood and adolescence in one line, the same kind of narrative silence all four Gospels keep about everyone's early years except this one infancy chapter's worth of material. His adult wilderness ministry, baptizing crowds in the Jordan and eventually clashing with Herod Antipas, begins only in Luke 3.

Cited in: Luke 1:5-25, 1:57-80.

This chapter adds: Josephus's stated motive for the execution (fear of rebellion, per Antiquities 18.5.2) diverges from the Gospels' Herodias-based motive, and names Machaerus as the corroborated, excavated site of the imprisonment/execution [100] [101].

[Beat 540]
JOSEPH (HUSBAND OF MARY)

Named at Luke 1:27 only as "a man whose name was Joseph, of the house of David," Mary's betrothed — Luke gives him no direct dialogue or independent scene in chapter 1, in sharp contrast to Matthew's account, where Joseph's perspective, doubts, and dream-guided decisions carry the entire nativity narrative (Matthew 1:18-25). Luke's "house of David" here does the same genealogical work Matthew's opening genealogy does explicitly (Matthew 1:1-16): it establishes, through Joseph's legal line, the Davidic descent Gabriel's own words to Mary invoke directly — "the Lord God will give to him the throne of his ancestor David" (Luke 1:32) — a claim resting on Joseph's paternity in the legal, adoptive sense rather than on any biological descent through Mary, since Luke's own narrative has Jesus conceived "by the Holy Spirit" with no human father (1:34-35). At the point Gabriel appears to Mary, Joseph and Mary are betrothed (erusin) but not yet living together as a completed marriage (nissuin) — a legally binding stage in its own right, not an informal understanding, which the text's own later crisis over Mary's pregnancy (handled in Matthew, not narrated in Luke's own chapter 1) depends on [9][10].

Cited in: Luke 1:27; see Notes 9, 10.

This chapter adds: his trade, tekton, most likely meant general builder/craftsman in wood or stone, not narrowly 'carpenter,' plausibly tied to the Sepphoris building economy [93].

[Beat 541]
JUDAS (SON OF JAMES) / THADDAEUS

Named 'Judas son of James' in Luke's two apostle lists (6:16; Acts 1:13); the parallel figure in Matthew and Mark's lists is named 'Thaddaeus' instead. The two names never appear together in a single source, making their conventional identification as one person an inference from list-position rather than a stated fact [86].

[Beat 542]
JUDAS ISCARIOT

The twelfth apostle named in Luke's roster (6:16), identified here — well before his later act — as 'who became a traitor.' The epithet 'Iscariot' is most plausibly derived from Hebrew Ish-Kerioth ('man of Kerioth,' a Judean town), against a minority theory linking it to the Latin sicarius, a theory chronologically difficult since the Sicarii as an organized group are attested only from the 40s-50s CE, after Judas's death [89].

This chapter adds: Luke 22:5-6 leaves the payment amount unspecified ("silver," no sum); the famous "thirty pieces of silver" is Matthew's detail alone (26:15). Also: Luke's specific framing that "Satan entered into Judas" before the betrayal negotiation (22:3) [266].

[Beat 543]
JUDEA

Named twice in Luke 1: as the seat of "King Herod of Judea" at the chapter's opening (1:5), and again as the location of "a Judean town in the hill country" (1:39) where Elizabeth and Zechariah live and where Mary visits, deliver the Magnificat, and stays roughly three months (1:56). The town itself is never named — only "the hill country of Judea," the elevated, village-dotted region surrounding Jerusalem — which has left its identification with any single modern site an open question rather than a settled one; church tradition since at least the Byzantine period has favored Ein Kerem, west of Jerusalem, but the text itself supplies no name to verify that identification against. Judea's political status under "King Herod" places these events within Herod the Great's reign (which the independent record dates to 37-4 BCE, based on Josephus's detailed political history of the period), the same ruler whose court and death Matthew's nativity account also centers its own chronology on.

Cited in: Luke 1:5, 1:39-56.

[Beat 544]
JULIUS AFRICANUS

An early third-century Christian writer whose letter to a correspondent named Aristides, preserved only because Eusebius later quoted it, contains the oldest attempt on record to reconcile Matthew's and Luke's conflicting genealogies of Jesus — a levirate-marriage reconstruction involving two half-brothers and a twice-married woman, offered as family history Africanus claimed came from Jesus's own surviving relatives, though no other source can confirm it.

Cited in: referenced in the genealogy harmonization discussion (Luke 3:23-38); see Note 44.

[Beat 545]
LAKE OF GENNESARET (SEA OF GALILEE / LAKE KINNERET)

The freshwater lake in Galilee where this chapter's opening fishing episode is set (5:1), called by Luke 'the lake of Gennesaret' and elsewhere in the Gospels the Sea of Galilee — modern Lake Kinneret. The lake's first-century fishing economy is independently documented as taxed and licensed under Herod Antipas [67], and a genuine first-century fishing vessel — the Kinneret Boat — was recovered from its bed in 1986 [66].

This chapter adds: a specific, independently attested meteorological mechanism (Golan cold-air drainage into the below-sea-level basin) explains the lake's documented sudden storms [114].

The Bethsaida site dispute (et-Tell vs. el-Araj) turns partly on each site's distance from the current shoreline, relevant background given the Jordan delta's known sedimentation and shoreline shift over two millennia [128].

[Beat 546]
LEGIO X FRETENSIS

A Roman legion whose well-documented regional presence dates to the First Jewish-Roman War (66-73 CE) and its aftermath; its unit emblems reportedly included a boar, connected by some scholars to the demon "Legion" and the swine of Luke 8:30-33 [120]. Its attested presence postdates Jesus's ministry by decades [121].

[Beat 547]
LEVI (TAX COLLECTOR)

The toll collector Jesus calls from his booth in this chapter (5:27-32), traditionally identified with the apostle Matthew under the theory that he held two names — an identification recent scholarship has directly challenged rather than confirmed [78]. His profession situates him within the real, historically attested Herodian tax-farming system operating at Capernaum, a town positioned on an internal border between two tetrarchies [77].

This chapter adds: named 'Matthew' in Luke's roster of the Twelve (6:15), a name divergence paralleling the Thaddaeus/Judas-son-of-James pattern seen elsewhere in the apostle lists [86].

[Beat 548]
LEVIRATE MARRIAGE

Deuteronomic legal custom (25:5-10) obligating a surviving brother to marry his deceased, childless brother's widow so the family line and inheritance would continue [256].

[Beat 549]
LOT

Figure from Genesis 19 invoked in Luke 17:28-32 ("remember Lot's wife") as a second type for sudden judgment amid ordinary life; tied to the unresolved Sodom-location debate [221].

[Beat 550]
LYSANIAS

Named by Luke as "tetrarch of Abilene" (3:1), a small territory in the Anti-Lebanon northwest of Damascus. For much of the nineteenth century this name looked like a clear historical error — the only independently documented Lysanias of that region had been executed decades before Luke's scene takes place — until an inscription surfaced naming a second, later Lysanias holding the same title in the correct period, resolving the objection in Luke's favor.

Cited in: Luke 3:1; see Note 38.

[Beat 551]
MACHAERUS

A Herodian fortress-palace east of the Dead Sea (in modern Jordan), independently named by Flavius Josephus as the site of John the Baptist's imprisonment and execution under Herod Antipas. Lost to memory after Rome destroyed it circa 71-72 CE, the site was securely reidentified in 1968 and has been under active excavation and partial reconstruction by Hungarian archaeologist Gyozo Voros since 2009 [101].

[Beat 552]
MAGDALA

A Galilean town on the western shore of the Sea of Galilee, site of a first-century synagogue excavated from 2009 onward, dated by a recovered coin to no later than 29 CE and destroyed around 67 CE; associated by name with Mary Magdalene [85].

[Beat 553]
MAMMON

Aramaic loanword (mamona) for wealth or possessions, left untranslated by Luke at 16:9, 11, and 13; treated almost as a rival power competing with God for loyalty rather than a neutral medium of exchange [206].

[Beat 554]
MARTHA (OF BETHANY)

A woman who welcomes Jesus into her home in an unnamed village and is later described as "distracted by all the preparations" while her sister Mary sits and listens to Jesus (10:38-42). Possibly, though not certainly, identical with the Martha of John 11-12 at Bethany [149] [150].

[Beat 555]
MARY (MOTHER OF JESUS)

Introduced in Luke's own account at 1:26-27 as "a virgin engaged to a man whose name was Joseph, of the house of David," living in Nazareth, at the moment Gabriel arrives to announce that she will conceive "by the Holy Spirit" (1:35) and bear a son to be named Jesus. Luke's introduction differs in emphasis from Matthew's (Matthew 1:18-25): Matthew's account centers on Joseph's perspective and his decision not to divorce Mary quietly; Luke's centers on Mary directly, gives her the extended dialogue with Gabriel (1:34-38) and, later, the Magnificat (1:46-55), and has her travel independently to Elizabeth's house in the Judean hill country (1:39-45). At the point Gabriel appears, Mary and Joseph are in the erusin stage of Jewish marriage — a formal, legally binding betrothal distinct from the completed marriage (nissuin) that would follow roughly a year later — which is what makes an unexplained pregnancy the specific crisis both nativity accounts treat it as, rather than an embarrassment between two people not yet bound to each other [9][10]. Her own hymn, the Magnificat, is close enough in structure and language to Hannah's Song (1 Samuel 2:1-10) that the mainstream historical-critical reading treats it as a pre-existing Jewish hymn Luke incorporated and placed in her mouth, rather than an unrehearsed composition spoken on the spot [12].

Cited in: Luke 1:26-56; see Notes 9, 10, 12.

This chapter adds: the perpetual-virginity tradition (Jerome vs. Helvidius) directly engages the "brothers" language of Luke 8:19-21 [113].

[Beat 556]
MARY (OF BETHANY)

Martha's sister, who "sat at the Lord's feet, listening to what he said" (10:39), a posture identified as the technical idiom for formal rabbinic discipleship in this period [147]. Distinguished from Mary the mother of Jesus and from Mary Magdalene.

[Beat 557]
MARY MAGDALENE

First named in Luke's own text at 8:2, as one of the women who traveled with Jesus and funded his ministry, described as a woman "from whom seven demons had gone out." Nothing in this or any other canonical text identifies her as a prostitute or links her to the unnamed sinful woman of Luke 7:36-50 or to Mary of Bethany; that composite reading originates with a sixth-century homily by Pope Gregory the Great and has been widely set aside by modern scholarship [109].

This chapter adds: named first among the empty-tomb witnesses in all four canonical gospels (24:1-12 and parallels); central figure in the "argument from embarrassment" discussion [296].

[Beat 558]
MICAH (PROPHET)

Eighth-century BCE Judean prophet, contemporary of Isaiah, author of the book bearing his name. Luke 12:53 directly quotes Micah 7:6's list of family members turned against one another, one of the more precisely traceable Old Testament citations in Luke's Gospel [175].

[Beat 559]
MINA

Greek/Hellenistic currency unit equal to 100 drachmas; distinct from and smaller than the "talent" (60 minas) used in Matthew's parallel parable of the talents [242].

[Beat 560]
MISHNAH

The foundational codification of Jewish oral law, redacted under Rabbi Judah ha-Nasi around 200 CE, roughly 170 years after the events of this chapter. Its Shabbat tractate (7:2) supplies the classic list of 39 categories of Sabbath-forbidden labor frequently — and somewhat anachronistically — read back into first-century Gospel Sabbath controversies [82].

This chapter cites tractate Demai and its content on doubtful-tithe produce law [159].

This chapter cites a specific new tractate and passage, Gittin 9:10 (the Hillel/Shammai/Akiva divorce debate) [208].

[Beat 561]
MOSES

Lawgiver and central figure of the Exodus tradition, whose death and burial location scripture leaves deliberately unrecorded (Deuteronomy 34:5-6). Appears alongside Elijah at the Transfiguration (9:30). His administrative advice from his father-in-law Jethro (Exodus 18:21) is also the likely background for the "groups of about fifty" detail in the feeding of the five thousand [132].

[Beat 562]
MOUNT HERMON

A roughly 2,814-meter massif at the northern edge of Israelite territory, just north of Caesarea Philippi, proposed by a number of modern scholars as the true site of the Transfiguration on the grounds of its height, seclusion, and geographic proximity to the location just named in the preceding pericope in Matthew and Mark [135].

[Beat 563]
MOUNT OF PRECIPICE (MT KEDUMIM)

A steep hillside south of Nazareth overlooking the Jezreel Valley, identified since the Byzantine period as the cliff to which the Nazareth crowd drove Jesus intending to throw him off (4:29). The earliest physical trace of the tradition is a sixth-century mosaic floor at the summit; Luke's own text specifies only `the brow of the hill on which their town was built,'' without naming a location [57].

[Beat 564]
MOUNT TABOR

A rounded, freestanding hill in the Jezreel Valley in lower Galilee, the earliest-attested pilgrimage identification for the Transfiguration's unnamed "high mountain," recorded by Origen in the third century and by Cyril of Jerusalem and Jerome in the fourth. The identification faces an archaeological objection: the summit carried a fortified garrison across the Hellenistic and Hasmonean periods and again in 66-67 CE [134].

[Beat 565]
NAIN

A small Galilean village, identified with the modern Arab village of Nein on the northwest slope of Jebel Dahi (Hill of Moreh), roughly 9-10 km southeast of Nazareth, and the setting of the widow's son's raising (7:11-17). The identification rests on continuous toponym survival and fourth-century patristic testimony, confirmed on the ground by nineteenth-century surveyors; the site has never been the subject of a full modern excavation [98].

[Beat 566]
NATHAN (SON OF DAVID)

A son of David born in Jerusalem, named only once in the Hebrew Bible's own record of David's children (2 Samuel 5:14) and never mentioned again anywhere in scripture outside that single genealogical list — until Luke's genealogy of Jesus runs its line of descent through him rather than through Solomon, the son Matthew's own genealogy uses (3:31; compare Matthew 1:6). Distinct from Nathan the prophet, a different figure entirely who confronts David over Bathsheba and Uriah in 2 Samuel 12.

Cited in: Luke 3:31; see Note 43.

[Beat 567]
NAZARETH

Named at Luke 1:26 as "a town in Galilee" and the place where Gabriel appears to Mary — the same small village Matthew's nativity account also gives as the family's origin point (Matthew 2:23), though the two Gospels disagree on how the family came to be living there by the time of Jesus's birth (Luke has them travel there from the start; Matthew's sequence implies a later relocation after Egypt). No source outside the New Testament — not the Hebrew Bible, not the Talmud, not Josephus, who wrote extensively about Galilee and names dozens of towns he personally fortified or campaigned near during the Jewish War a generation later — mentions Nazareth before the Gospels do. Archaeological survey has recovered agricultural installations consistent with the right period (cisterns, storage pits, terraced hillside plots, rock-cut tombs) but no public architecture, wall circuit, or any marker of an administrative or commercial center; working population estimates put first-century Nazareth at roughly 400 residents on about 60 acres of hillside, against an estimated 8,000 to 12,000 at Sepphoris, the rebuilt Roman regional capital six kilometers away that Herod Antipas was actively expanding during these same years [11]. Nothing about the site's archaeology or its total absence from earlier sources contradicts Luke's account; it simply confirms that Nazareth really was the marginal, unremarkable place both Gospels independently describe it as.

Cited in: Luke 1:26; see Note 11.

This chapter adds: its economic base was plausibly linked to unskilled and craft labor for Herod Antipas's Sepphoris building boom roughly four miles away [93].

[Beat 568]
NAZARETH INSCRIPTION

A marble slab inscribed in Greek with an imperial edict — headed diatagma Kaisaros, ordinance of Caesar — forbidding the disturbance of tombs and prescribing a capital charge for removing a buried body. Bought in 1878 by the antiquarian Wilhelm Froehner with a note saying only that it came from Nazareth, and never excavated. For most of the twentieth century it was offered as documentary corroboration of the empty-tomb controversy. In 2020 stable-isotope analysis matched its marble to a quarry on the Greek island of Kos, removing the basis for the Nazareth attribution and suggesting a different occasion entirely: the desecrated tomb of Nikias, a tyrant of Kos. Still a real Roman edict about grave-robbing; no longer evidence about this story.

[Beat 569]
NOAH

Figure from Genesis 6-9 invoked in Luke 17:26-27 as a type for the Kingdom's sudden, unsignaled arrival; connects to this book's existing Gilgamesh/flood-tradition material [223].

[Beat 570]
PAPYRUS 75 (P75)

Third-century New Testament manuscript whose 1961 publication sided with the longer text at several disputed Luke 24 readings, undermining the premise of the "Western non-interpolations" theory [301].

[Beat 571]
PHILIP (APOSTLE)

One of the Twelve, named in Luke's roster (6:14) and in all four New Testament lists, first appearing here with no prior introduction in Luke. There is no independent historical record of his activity beyond the Gospel and Acts material; the later, non-canonical Acts of Philip (probably 4th-century) supplies extensive legendary detail about his missionary career that cannot be independently verified and postdates him by centuries.

[Beat 572]
PHILIP THE TETRARCH

Another son of Herod the Great, and Antipas's half-brother, who received the northeastern quarter of their father's former kingdom — Ituraea, Trachonitis, and neighboring territory — in the same 4 BCE division (3:1). Philip is the one ruler in Luke's whole synchronism with direct physical self-attestation: coins struck in his own name, bearing dated regnal years, survive and confirm a roughly thirty-seven-year reign, longer than either of his better-known brothers.

Cited in: Luke 3:1; see Note 37.

[Beat 573]
PONTIUS PILATE

Prefect of Judea from roughly 26 to 36 CE, named here only as part of Luke's opening date-stamp (3:1) — his actual trial scene doesn't arrive until chapter 23. Nothing about his life before this appointment survives in any source, and the only artifact naming him at all is a damaged inscription found in 1961 at Caesarea Maritima, confirming his real title was prefect rather than the "procurator" later writers like Tacitus use. Independent of any Gospel, Josephus and Philo of Alexandria both describe a harsh, often violent governor — standard-bearing riots, an aqueduct funded from Temple money, a Samaritan massacre that finally got him recalled to Rome — a portrait considerably less sympathetic than the reluctant, hand-wringing Pilate later Gospel tradition builds toward.

Cited in: Luke 3:1; see Notes 35, 36.

This chapter adds the Galileans-killed-at-sacrifice incident (13:1), flagged explicitly as attested nowhere outside Luke, alongside Josephus's three independently-attested Pilate atrocities (standards, aqueduct riot, Mount Gerizim massacre) [179] [178].

This chapter adds: the three-charge accusation (23:2) and its mapping onto real Roman legal categories (seditio for the tax/subversion charges, maiestas for the kingship charge) [281].

[Beat 574]
POOL OF SILOAM

A Second Temple-period stepped pool excavated in Jerusalem in 2004 by archaeologists Ronny Reich and Eli Shukron, dated by coins of Alexander Jannaeus and Herodian-era pottery [180]. Located near the tower referenced in Luke 13:4.

[Beat 575]
PRIVILEGIUM PASCHALE

Scholarly term for the disputed Passover prisoner-release custom depicted in all four Gospels; no independent Jewish or Roman source documents such a standing amnesty [284].

[Beat 576]
PSALM 110

Royal enthronement psalm quoted by Jesus in the "whose son is the Christ" exchange (20:41-44); Davidic authorship disputed in critical scholarship [257].

[Beat 577]
QUIRINIUS

Publius Sulpicius Quirinius, named at Luke 2:2 as governor of Syria at the time of the census Luke ties to Jesus's birth. Quirinius is a real, independently documented Roman official — consul in 12 BCE, later a military commander in Asia Minor — whose governorship of Syria and accompanying census of newly annexed Judea are dated precisely by Josephus to 6 CE, the same census Luke's own later book, Acts 5:37, references by name in connection with the revolt it provoked. This creates the single most load-bearing chronological problem in the entire nativity: Matthew's own account requires Jesus to be born no later than 4 BCE (before Herod the Great's death), while Luke's dating requires a census conducted no earlier than 6 CE under Quirinius — a gap of roughly a decade between the two Gospels' own internal chronologies, not a contradiction manufactured by an outside skeptic. No independent source attests Quirinius holding any Syrian post before 6 CE, despite proposed harmonizations built on a fragmentary, unidentified inscription (the Tiburtine Inscription) describing an official who governed Syria twice. This project treats the gap as a genuine, unresolved discrepancy rather than a solved puzzle — see the full treatment and sourcing in this chapter's own beat and Notes 16-22.

Cited in: Luke 2:2; see Notes 16, 18, 20, 21.

[Beat 578]
QUMRAN COMMUNITY (DEAD SEA SCROLLS)

The Second Temple-era Jewish sectarian community whose surviving scrolls describe organizing both their idealized future congregation and their war-camp regulations into administrative units of thousands, hundreds, fifties, and tens [132] — independent material evidence that this numerical organizational grid was live administrative vocabulary in the period.

[Beat 579]
ROYAL STOA / ROBINSONS ARCH

Monumental colonnaded hall and its connecting staircase-arch on Herod's Temple platform; archaeologically attested; backdrop for the scale of commercial activity Jesus disrupts in the Temple cleansing [249].

[Beat 580]
SAMARIA

The mixed-population territory north of Judea and south of Galilee, its border with Galilee a real administrative and cultural seam Jesus crosses repeatedly on the road to Jerusalem [220].

[Beat 581]
SAMARITANS

An ethno-religious community centered on Mount Gerizim in Samaria, sharing scriptural roots with Judean and Galilean Jews but divided from them over the legitimate site of worship and, practically, over safe use of the direct Galilee-to-Jerusalem pilgrimage road through Samaritan territory. Josephus documents a real, violent escalation along that road around 52 CE [136].

This chapter adds: the Good Samaritan parable's hero is identified only by this group membership (10:33), against a documented backdrop of mutual hostility that coexisted with practical travel through Samaritan territory [144].

[Beat 582]
SANHEDRIN

Jerusalem's seventy-one-member governing council of chief priests, elders, and scribes under Roman-era Judea; the coalition that challenges Jesus's authority in the Temple (20:1) [250].

[Beat 583]
SEPPHORIS

A city roughly four miles from Nazareth, rebuilt by Herod Antipas in an extravagant Roman architectural style as his first Galilean capital, driving a major construction boom spanning roughly the first three decades of the first century CE — plausibly the economic backdrop to Nazareth's craftsman households, including Joseph's and Jesus's own building trade [93].

[Beat 584]
SHEOL / HADES (SECOND TEMPLE COSMOLOGY)

The Jewish underworld concept, morally neutral and undivided in the earlier Hebrew Bible but reimagined by the Second Temple period (e.g., 1 Enoch 22) as compartmentalized into separate chambers for righteous and wicked dead, separated by chasms, light, and water [214].

[Beat 585]
SHEPHERDS (LUKE 2)

The unnamed shepherds "living in the fields, keeping watch over their flock by night" near Bethlehem (2:8) who receive the angelic announcement of Jesus's birth — "an angel of the Lord" followed by "a multitude of the heavenly host" — and become the first people outside the family to see the child (2:8-20). Shepherding was a genuinely real, low-status, itinerant occupation in this period, structurally prone to a specific suspicion: a shepherd grazing someone else's flock had every opportunity to let the animals wander onto land that wasn't his, the exact concern the Babylonian Talmud raises, centuries later, in its own discussion of trades disqualifying a man from serving as a court witness. That detail has been considerably overstated in popular preaching into the claim, repeated from many pulpits, that first-century shepherds were formally unclean, socially despised outcasts, and legally barred from testifying in court at all — a claim traced most often to Joachim Jeremias's mid-century social history, whose own supporting citations, on inspection, are drawn almost entirely from the Mishnah and Talmud, legal texts compiled two to five centuries after the events Luke describes. No first-century source independently confirms shepherds as a formally excluded class; the Hebrew Bible made shepherding the trade of both Moses and King David without any hint of disgrace, and Luke's own text narrates the shepherds' report as credible ("all who heard it were amazed," 2:18) rather than suspect. The honest reading: genuinely poor and itinerant, plausibly the target of exactly the property-dispute suspicion the later rabbinic sources record — but not the formally unclean, court-barred outcasts the popular version claims.

Cited in: Luke 2:8-20; see Notes 24, 25, 26.

[Beat 586]
SIMEON (JERUSALEM)

A "righteous and devout" man in Jerusalem, introduced at Luke 2:25 as "looking forward to the consolation of Israel," who takes the infant Jesus in his arms at the Temple and delivers the hymn traditionally known as the Nunc Dimittis ("Lord, now you are letting your servant depart in peace," 2:29-32) before blessing the family and warning Mary that "a sword will pierce your own soul" (2:35). Nothing outside Luke's own text identifies Simeon further — no surname, no office, no independent record of him exists. Raymond Brown's standard critical commentary on the infancy narratives reads Simeon, alongside Anna, as a literary embodiment of the anawim — the pious "poor of Israel" awaiting divine redemption, a devotional posture with some resonance in other Second Temple-period religious writing — rather than as a figure whose biographical particulars the text is chiefly concerned with documenting. Appears only in Luke, entirely within this chapter.

Cited in: Luke 2:25-35; see Note 28.

[Beat 587]
SIMON (PETER)

A resident of Capernaum whose house Jesus enters and whose mother-in-law he heals of a high fever (4:38-39); this is Simon's first appearance in Luke's narrative, ahead of his formal call to discipleship in the following chapter. He is not yet given the added name `Peter'' here. The `House of Peter'' identified by twentieth-century excavation at Capernaum is a later, separately argued archaeological claim about his residence, not something the text itself locates for the reader [59].

This chapter adds: called by Jesus from a working fishing partnership with James and John, sons of Zebedee, on the lake of Gennesaret (5:1-11); Luke's description of him as a working fisherman under a taxed, licensed lake economy is well attested by the material and economic record even though no source outside the Christian tradition independently names him [67].

New confession content: "The Christ of God" (9:20) is Peter's own answer to Jesus's question of identity, a milestone in his characterization.

This chapter adds: uniquely named (with John) as the disciple sent ahead to prepare the Passover (22:8), where Mark/Matthew say only "his disciples"; also the specific three-exchange denial sequence and rooster-crow timing (22:54-62) [279].

[Beat 588]
SIMON (THE PHARISEE, LUKE 7)

The otherwise-unidentified Pharisee host named 'Simon' at Luke 7:40, in whose house the sinful woman anoints Jesus's feet. Nothing external corroborates this individual; he is known only from this narrative, and, per the synoptic-identity debate, is a different figure from 'Simon the leper,' host of the later Bethany anointing in Mark and Matthew [105].

Cross-reference: Mary Magdalene's clean, distinct naming at Luke 8:2 is the textual anchor against which this entry's discussion of the Gregory the Great conflation links forward [109].

[Beat 589]
SIMON CALLED THE ZEALOT

One of the Twelve, named in Luke's roster (6:15) with the epithet ho zelotes; called 'the Cananaean' in Mark's parallel list. The title most likely describes personal religious zeal rather than membership in the organized Zealot political-military faction, which did not coalesce under that name until the 60s CE, decades after Jesus's ministry [87] [88].

[Beat 590]
SIMON OF CYRENE

North African Jewish diaspora bystander pressed to carry the crossbeam to the execution site (23:26) [286] [287].

[Beat 591]
SODOM

The city destroyed in Genesis 19, named directly in Luke 17:29; its archaeological location remains genuinely disputed among Bab edh-Dhra/Numeira, Tall el-Hammam, and unconfirmed alternatives [221] [222].

[Beat 592]
SOLOMON

David's son by Bathsheba, and the ancestor through whom Matthew's genealogy of Jesus runs (Matthew 1:6) — the point of sharpest divergence from Luke's own genealogy in this chapter, which instead descends through Nathan, a different son of David entirely (3:31). The two Gospels agree Jesus's legal line passes through David; they disagree, from Solomon's generation onward, on almost every name in between.

Cited in: referenced in comparison to Luke's own genealogy (3:23-31); see Note 43.

This chapter adds a new literary use: Luke 12:27 invokes Solomon's proverbial splendor specifically as a clothing/adornment comparison ('Solomon in all his glory was not arrayed like one of these [lilies]').

[Beat 593]
STEWARD (OIKONOMOS)

A real Greco-Roman administrative office: a manager delegated authority over an estate's accounts, leases, wages, and produce on behalf of an absentee owner, attested in surviving Roman-period Egyptian estate archives [203].

[Beat 594]
SUSANNA (LUKE 8)

Named once, at Luke 8:3, among the women who financially supported Jesus's traveling ministry. She receives no further description or mention anywhere else in the New Testament.

[Beat 595]
SYCAMORE-FIG TREE (FICUS SYCOMORUS)

Low-branching, climbable fig-mulberry native to the Jordan Valley/Jericho region; the tree Zacchaeus climbs; botanically distinct from the "sycamine" (black mulberry, Morus nigra) named in Luke 17:6 [241].

[Beat 596]
SYNAGOGUE (FIRST-CENTURY)

The Jewish communal institution for Sabbath assembly, Torah and Prophets reading, and teaching, featured in both the Nazareth (4:16-30) and Capernaum (4:31-37) scenes. In this period a synagogue could be either a purpose-built structure or simply a designated meeting space; a fixed weekly lectionary pairing specific Torah and Prophets readings is documented only in later rabbinic-era sources [53]. Ten-plus purpose-built synagogue buildings dated before 70 CE have been excavated in Judea and Galilee, but none yet at Nazareth [55].

This chapter adds: the archisynagogos title held by Jairus is epigraphically attested pre-70 CE by the Theodotos inscription [124].

[Beat 597]
THE DEVIL (SATAN)

The tempter figure who confronts Jesus in the wilderness across all three temptations (4:1-13), described by Luke as `the devil'' and departing `until an opportune time,'' a phrase that signals his later reappearance in the Passion narrative. The scene's literary construction — built around a scripted exchange of scriptural citations rather than a plausible transcript — is well attested [48], but nothing about the tempter's nature or reality is a claim external evidence could speak to one way or the other.

[Beat 598]
THE PHARISEES

A pietist Jewish movement of the late Second Temple period, first appearing by name in this book's narrative in this chapter as objectors to Jesus' forgiveness claim (5:21) and his choice of dinner company (5:30). Independently attested by Josephus as one of several identifiable schools of thought, though modern scholarship cautions against assuming they were as large or uniformly influential as later tradition suggests [74]. Known independently for practices including twice-weekly fasting, corroborated outside the Gospels by the early Christian Didache [79].

This chapter adds: Jacob Neusner's count of pre-70 traditions attributed to the Pharisees found the large majority concerned with food-purity practice rather than an elaborate Sabbath-labor code, and the fully systematized 39-category Sabbath framework often read into scenes like Luke 6:1-5 wasn't compiled until the Mishnah, around 200 CE [82] [83].

This chapter adds a corroborating claim (Mishnah tractate Demai, tithing scrupulosity over minor garden herbs) that substantiates rather than caricatures the group's documented practice [159].

This chapter adds: "one of the chief Pharisees" hosts Jesus for a Sabbath meal (14:1), the setting for both the dropsy healing and the banquet-humility teaching; also affirmed bodily resurrection per Josephus (Antiquities 18.14) [189].

This chapter adds: standing was the normal, non-distinctive Temple prayer posture, not a Pharisaic affectation, per Mishnah Berakhot [230].

[Beat 599]
THE SADDUCEES

Second Temple Jewish sect, priestly-aristocratic in composition, that denied bodily resurrection, angels, and spirits, in contrast to the Pharisees. Attested independently by Josephus (Antiquities 18.16; Jewish War 2.164-165) and by Acts 23:8, making the Pharisee-Sadducee resurrection split one of the best-corroborated points of internal Jewish theological diversity in this period [189]. Luke 14:14's "resurrection of the just" assumes the Pharisaic side of this exact debate.

This chapter adds: their resurrection challenge (20:27-40) is built specifically on the Deuteronomy 25:5-10 levirate marriage law [256].

[Beat 600]
THE SCRIBES

A trained clerical-legal professional class appearing in this chapter (5:21, 5:30) paired narratively with the Pharisees, though the two were not necessarily the same people. Josephus does not treat scribes as a distinct sect; the term denotes a documented governmental and legal-clerical function operating at multiple levels of Second Temple Jewish administration [74].

This chapter adds: accused in 20:45-47 of seeking public honor and of "devouring widows' houses" — a rhetorical accusation, not an independently attested historical pattern.

[Beat 601]
THE SEVENTY(-TWO)

The group of disciples Jesus appoints and sends out in pairs ahead of him (10:1), distinct from the twelve apostles named earlier in Luke. The Greek manuscript tradition is evenly split between "seventy" and "seventy-two" [137], a number likely carrying symbolic weight from the seventy elders of Numbers 11 and the nations of Genesis 10 [138].

[Beat 602]
THE TEMPLE (JERUSALEM)

The Second Temple, at this point in its Herodian expansion (Herod the Great's massive rebuilding and enlargement of the Temple Mount began around 20-19 BCE and continued for decades), is the setting for two separate scenes in this chapter: the infant Jesus's presentation and the family's purification offering (2:22-38), and, eleven years or so later, the twelve-year-old Jesus sitting among the teachers, "listening to them and asking them questions" (2:41-51). The building's physical existence, location, and Herodian-era expansion are independently and extensively documented — by Josephus's detailed descriptions, by the Temple Mount's surviving retaining walls (including the Western Wall), and by archaeological work across the site and its surrounding plazas. What is not independently checkable is the specific dialogue and detail of either Temple scene in Luke — no outside source records Simeon, Anna, or the boy Jesus's exchange with the Temple teachers; both scenes rest on Luke's own narrative alone. The Temple would be destroyed by Roman forces in 70 CE, within the lifetime of Luke's earliest readers — a fact Luke's Gospel elsewhere seems to write with awareness of, though neither Temple scene in this chapter references that future destruction.

Cited in: Luke 2:22-38, 2:41-51.

This chapter adds two new checkable claims: the Tyrian-shekel purity requirement behind Temple money-changing [247], and the archaeologically-attested scale of the Royal Stoa/Robinson's Arch complex as backdrop for the cleansing [249].
