This chapter adds: "officers of the temple guard" (22:4, 22:52) named as the enforcement arm involved in negotiating with Judas and in Jesus's arrest.

This chapter adds: Joseph of Arimathea's status as "a member of the council" who dissented from the verdict (23:50-51), a documented instance of internal Sanhedrin non-unanimity.

[Beat 603]
THEOPHILUS

Named at Luke 1:3 as the recipient to whom Luke addresses his "orderly account," and again at Acts 1:1, addressed by the same title without repeating his name — the two books read as a single two-volume work dedicated to one person. The title Luke gives him, "most excellent" (Greek kratistos), appears exactly twice more anywhere in the New Testament, both times used to address Roman governors, Felix (Acts 23:26) and Festus (Acts 24:3), which some scholars take as evidence Theophilus held genuine social or official rank and may have been the patron whose resources funded Luke's research and writing [5]. "Theophilus" is independently attested as an ordinary Greek personal name in documented use since at least the third century BCE, which weighs against, without disproving, the alternative reading proposed as early as the third-century church father Origen: that the name — literally "friend of God" — functions as an address to any reader who fits that description rather than naming one historical patron [5]. Scripture gives no other information about him; everything beyond the two addresses is inference.

Cited in: Luke 1:3; see Note 5.

[Beat 604]
THOMAS (APOSTLE)

One of the Twelve, named in Luke's roster (6:15). No independent first-century source corroborates his individual activity; his later association with the apocryphal Gospel of Thomas and the Acts of Thomas (both non-canonical, dated well into the 2nd-3rd centuries) is legendary accretion, not attested history.

[Beat 605]
TIBERIUS CAESAR

Roman emperor from 14 to 37 CE, and the reigning emperor for the entirety of Jesus's adult ministry and crucifixion, though he never appears in any Gospel scene directly — his name functions here purely as a dating anchor, "the fifteenth year of the reign of Tiberius Caesar" (3:1), fixing John's ministry to roughly 28-29 CE by the reckoning ancient Roman historians themselves used, with a real minority scholarly dispute over an alternate co-regency count shifting the date a year or two earlier. He succeeded his stepfather Augustus, spent much of his later reign away from Rome on Capri, and left day-to-day government of the empire's eastern provinces — including the Judean prefecture Pontius Pilate held — largely to his own appointees.

Cited in: Luke 3:1; see Note 34.

This chapter adds: the specific denarius inscription associated with his reign (TI CAESAR DIVI AVG F AVGVSTVS) asserting divine filiation from the deified Augustus [254].

[Beat 606]
TOWER OF SILOAM

A structure in Jerusalem whose collapse killed eighteen people, cited by Jesus as an example of death without special culpability (13:4). A separate excavation identified fortification towers near the Pool of Siloam, raised in the Hellenistic period and rebuilt in the early Roman era, as a plausible but not certain match [181].

[Beat 607]
TRIBE OF ASHER

One of the twelve tribes of Israel, descended from Jacob's son Asher, and one of the ten northern tribes deported by the Assyrian empire in 722 BCE after the fall of Samaria (2 Kings 17:6) — conventionally grouped among the ten "lost tribes," since no historical record traces their subsequent population as a distinct, locatable group. Luke identifies the prophet Anna as being "of the tribe of Asher" (2:36), the tribe's only appearance in the New Testament tied to a specific named individual. By the first century CE, only Judah, Benjamin, and Levi had any plausible continuous genealogical trail back to a specific pre-exilic tribe; Josephus, writing near the same period, still insisted the ten deported tribes survived as "an immense multitude, not to be estimated by numbers," living somewhere "beyond the Euphrates" — a claim no independent source has ever located, counted, or corroborated. A first-century individual's claim to descent from Asher specifically, like Anna's, is best understood as an inherited family tradition of uncertain reliability rather than an independently checkable genealogical fact — not because the claim is necessarily false, but because nothing outside the claim itself could confirm or refute it by Luke's own time.

Cited in: Luke 2:36; see Notes 29, 30; see ANNA (PROPHET).

[Beat 608]
TYRIAN PURPLE

A luxury dye extracted from the glands of murex sea snails, requiring tens of thousands of snails and roughly ten days of processing per small batch; valued in antiquity at roughly its weight in gold, used in Luke 16:19 as an unambiguous top-of-the-economy status marker [213].

[Beat 609]
TYRIAN SHEKEL

High-purity silver coin minted at Tyre, bearing the pagan god Melqart; mandated for the half-shekel Temple tax despite its imagery because of its silver purity; created the need for Temple money-changers [247].

[Beat 610]
TZARAAT / LEPRA (LEPROSY)

The Hebrew and Greek terms rendered 'leprosy' in English translations of both Leviticus 13-14 and Luke 5:12-16, denoting a broad category of visible skin conditions rather than the specific bacterial illness modern medicine calls leprosy (Hansen's disease) [70]. The associated priestly purity-exclusion procedure is independently corroborated both earlier, at Qumran [72], and later, in the Mishnah [71].

[Beat 611]
VESPASIAN

Roman general and later emperor (r. 69-79 CE), reported by the Roman senator and historian Tacitus to have healed a blind man and a man with a withered hand at Alexandria through touch, acting on instructions the two petitioners said came from the god Serapis. Cited as an independent, non-Jewish, non-Christian parallel for miraculous-healing claims circulating about a public figure within living memory of the events [62].

[Beat 612]
WADI QELT / ASCENT OF ADUMMIM

The steep desert gorge and adjoining pass the ancient Jerusalem-Jericho road followed, descending nearly a thousand meters over the route's length. Independently documented as bandit-prone across antiquity, evidenced by successive fortified way stations built and rebuilt along it [142].

[Beat 613]
WESTERN NON-INTERPOLATIONS

Term coined by B. F. Westcott and F. J. A. Hort (1881) for nine short New Testament passages, eight of them in Luke 22-24, that the earliest manuscripts they judged most reliable omit or shorten relative to the majority text [301].

[Beat 614]
ZACCHAEUS

Jericho's chief tax collector (architelones); climbs a sycamore-fig tree to see Jesus; his name means "pure" in Hebrew, an irony against his profession [240] [239].

[Beat 615]
ZEBEDEE

The father of James and John, named only in passing here as the man whose sons are Simon's fishing partners (5:10). The broader pattern of Galilean fishing households pooling boats, nets, and labor is independently documented [68].

[Beat 616]
ZECHARIAH (PRIEST)

Introduced at Luke 1:5 as "a priest named Zechariah, who belonged to the priestly division of Abijah," husband of Elizabeth and father of John the Baptist. The "division of Abijah" places him within the twenty-four rotating priestly courses (mishmarot) that organized Second Temple service, a roster first listed in 1 Chronicles 24:7-19 (Abijah eighth) and independently corroborated among the Dead Sea Scrolls' own calendrical documents, including 4Q321, which tracks the same course names rotating through Temple duty on the Qumran community's separate calendar [6][7]. While serving his division's turn in the Temple, Zechariah is visited by the angel Gabriel, who announces that Elizabeth will bear a son despite her age and lifelong barrenness (1:8-20); Zechariah's doubt costs him his speech until the child's birth and naming (1:20, 1:64), after which he delivers the hymn known as the Benedictus (1:67-79). This Zechariah is distinct from "Zechariah, son of Barachiah," the murdered prophet Jesus references at Matthew 23:35 and Luke 11:51 — the two figures share a common name but nothing else, a point worth stating plainly since English translations give no marker to separate them. Appears only in Luke, entirely within chapter 1.

Cited in: Luke 1:5-25, 1:57-80; see Notes 6, 7.
